 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]

 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item {\csname fa0045Font0\endcsname\char"0057} neural language model is trained on the phrases ``small red car'' and ``large blue truck.''  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item The model stores all observed word pairs explicitly
     \item Embeddings capture semantics that combine compositionally
     \item The softmax layer guarantees coverage of unseen sequences
     \item Training implicitly includes all possible word permutations
 \end{enumerate}

 \item {\csname fa0079Font0\endcsname\char"0057}{\csname fa0079Font1\endcsname\char"0068}{\csname fa0079Font2\endcsname\char"0069} {\csname fa0080Font0\endcsname\char"0063}{\csname fa0080Font1\endcsname\char"0068}{\csname fa0080Font2\endcsname\char"0020} {\csname fa0081Font0\endcsname\char"0062}{\csname fa0081Font1\endcsname\char"0065}{\csname fa0081Font2\endcsname\char"006E} parameters across time steps. Compared to a model with independent parameters at each step, weight reuse primarily:
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item Allows parallel computation across sequence positions
     \item Eliminates vanishing gradients through parameter averaging
     \item Increases model capacity by expanding representational depth
     \item Reduces parameters and shares the same transformation
 \end{enumerate}

fa0194Font4\endcsname\char"003F}
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item Lower perplexity always indicates overfitting to training data
     \item Perplexity ignores normalization across token positions
     \item Question answering requires larger vocabularies than modeling
     \item Perplexity reflects intrinsic likelihood rather than task utility
 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item {\csname fa0195Font0\endcsname\char"0057}{\csname fa0195Font1\endcsname\char"0068} training, a neural network's loss decreases for a few iterations, then begins oscillating widely without converging. Which explanation is most consistent with this behavior?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item The batch size is too small, increasing gradient variability
     \item The batch size is too large, reducing gradient noise
     \item The learning rate is too small to escape local minima
     \item The learning rate is too large for the local loss curvature
 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item Consider a 4-word sequence $W = (w_1, w_2, w_3, w_4)$ with probabilities
 \[
 P(w_1) = 0.5,\quad
 P(w_2 \mid w_1) = 0.4,\quad
 P(w_3 \mid w_1, w_2) = 0.2,\quad
 P(w_4 \mid w_1, w_2, w_3) = 0.1.
 \]
 What is the perplexity of the sequence?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item $4.215$
     \item $2.154$
     \item $5.000$
     \item $3.976$
 \end{enumerate}
 
 \item A system has two states, $A$ and $B$, with the following transition probabilities:
 \begin{itemize}
     \item If in $A$: stays in $A$ with probability $0.6$ (and goes to $B$ with probability $0.4$)
     \item If in $B$: stays in $B$ with probability $0.4$ (and goes to $A$ with probability $0.6$)
 \end{itemize}
 Suppose the system starts in state $B$. As the number of transitions approaches infinity, what is the most accurate statement?
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\Alph*)]
     \item The state probabilities converge to a fixed stationary distribution
     \item The state probabilities continue fluctuating without convergence
     \item The limiting distribution depends on the initial state
     \item The chain eventually remains permanently in one state
 \end{enumerate}
